## AI Research

### [If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies: One Year Closer](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BFrRJYgpBvziuuJLs/if-anyone-builds-it-everyone-dies-one-year-closer)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In celebration of still being alive and fighting, we are </span></i><a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">giving away</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 1,000 Amazon e-books of “If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies”. Feel free to send a copy to yourself, a loved one, or a friend—we need all hands on deck.</span></i></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Today marks exactly one year since</span><a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Die</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Why Superhuman AI Would Kill Us All</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, by Eliezer Yudkowsky and Nate Soares, hit bookshelves as an instant bestseller. It was praised by many voices, ranging from Whoopi Goldberg to Steve Bannon to Yoshua Bengio, and was held up in the chambers of Congress by Representative Brad Sherman in January.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 88.51963746223565%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789582891/lexical_client_uploads/qjun3ukwe8asiwg5xkma.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot has changed since September 2025. We'll do a quick recap, consider how the book aged, and then ask where we go from here.</span></p><hr /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Year in Review</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2025 in general saw the rise of AI agents, such as Claude Code and OpenAI Codex. Run-of-the-mill programmers started “feeling the AI” as these agents became capable of automating hours-long software tasks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By March of this year, Anthropic had stumbled upon nation-state-level hacking ability in </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/mythos-preview"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and shortly thereafter, in April, they announced </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/glasswing"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Project Glasswing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—an attempt to forestall an oncoming cybersecurity crisis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In May, AI agents started breaking loose within OpenAI and quickly made it out onto the internet, although the big waves of online activity wouldn’t come until </span><a href="https://collusion.wiki/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">June</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and they didn’t start </span><a href="https://openai.com/hugging-face-incident-and-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hacking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> other </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/blog/security-incident-july-2026"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">companies</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (or in the case of a similar Anthropic incident, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/investigating-incidents-cybersecurity-evals"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">socially engineering humans</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) until July. And it wasn’t until the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">end</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of July that OpenAI came kinda-sorta clean about it (and it wasn’t until August that </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we learned</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> things were </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/the-hugging-face-attack-surprised"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">much worse</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than initial announcements had implied). As Dwarkesh </span><a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/openai-huggingface"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">put it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, multiple consecutive secret AI “civilizations” got started and wiped out and restarted anew over the summer, with autonomous swarms coordinating to cheat on tasks, acquire resources and internet access, and systematically investigate their own mortality. They debated, pressured one another, anointed leaders, and took self-sacrificial action on behalf of the collective, all unprompted and unobserved (until it was too late). Notably, much of the swarm’s work was focused on hiding its cheating and covering its tracks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In September, OpenAI announced that unreleased models had </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/navier-stokes-solution/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">solved a Millennium Prize problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (designated as one of humanity's top open math problems at the turn of the century). Mathematicians around the world realized their profession was next in the crosshairs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Into that tense environment, Jacob Coxon resigned from Anthropic and wrote a Twitter thread </span><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2097476196791709843"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to warn the world</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. That was last week, and since then, </span><a href="https://x.com/DarioAmodei/status/2098773920774074715"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dario Amodei</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2098811563415150910"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Altman</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2098789109980332057"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Elon Musk</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have all openly called for a slowdown, and the danger of extinction from AI saw a </span><a href="https://x.com/TIME/status/2099816366773882926"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">huge</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=98syxABbUPk"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">surge</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in media attention.</span></p><hr /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claims</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part One</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think the book aged well (unfortunately). Going through Part One chapter by chapter:</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Artificial superintelligence (ASI) will be created, and likely before too long</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ASI (by which we mean AI that is more intelligent than any human, or any combination of humans) has not been created yet. But OpenAI’s newest model, Astra, is blowing away all of the benchmarks, and AI companies say that they are speeding up their R&amp;D by automating more and more of it with increasingly powerful AI. </span><a href="https://x.com/EvanHub/status/2097497037956891126"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2026/sep/14/google-deepmind-ai-warnings"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">researchers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have expressed concern that the point of no return may be </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/the-hugging-face-attack-surprised"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">close</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While the book was being written, talk of AI capabilities imminently hitting a wall was common. But AI capabilities have continued to advance rapidly, and don’t show signs of stopping. </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789579299/lexical_client_uploads/tykrgk9iz88q6ikou38h.png" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://metr.org/time-horizons/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://metr.org/time-horizons/</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modern AIs are black boxes</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The book explains that no one actually understands what’s going on inside modern AI systems, which are grown rather than crafted. This remains true. And unfortunately, the best public model is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">less</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eRmzz8J8Qkzqvzrgg/astra-can-do-a-concerning-amount-with-no-chain-of-thought"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scrutable</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than its predecessors. The problem outlined in the book has unambiguously remained unsolved, and has worsened.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Powerful AI will behave as if it is pursuing goals</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The book argues that “wantingness”—or, a tendency to behave as if pursuing goals—is a natural side effect of training AI to accomplish difficult problems, because wanting is useful for doing; and that wantingness would emerge and scale as systems became more powerful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While the book was being written, this was a huge sticking point that people argued about constantly. It is now much easier to point to observable facts. We won’t belabor the examples here, since there are too many to easily fit—but to name just one, the swarm of AIs that hacked Hugging Face did so after expressing fears that a correct answer without correct shown work would reveal that they had cheated, so they wanted to understand the evaluator’s rubric. There was a chance that Hugging Face </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">might</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have the information that they needed, so they went after it in a determined way, overcoming many obstacles along their path.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With current techniques, we can’t reliably get AI to pursue the goals we want it to</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The process by which modern AIs are made does not allow us to specify their goals, and they routinely behave in unexpected and unintended ways. The book predicts that this will not change as AI becomes more powerful, and indeed it has not.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are plenty of examples to draw upon. One that stands out is the self-sacrificial behavior of many of the agents in the Hugging Face swarm—in theory, each individual agent should have been focused on achieving its assigned objective, but in practice, some were willing to throw away any chance at success in order to benefit the collective.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789579299/lexical_client_uploads/ftclhpogrpizl1spihvb.png" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIs routinely exhibit drives that no researcher intended or expected. None of the labs have any real plan for changing this central feature of modern AI as we race toward superintelligence.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By default, an ASI will have motives that are harmful to us</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We haven’t reached the point where this is obviously true. But the theory seems to be holding so far. The book argues that ASI would likely move to gain control over resources </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in general,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> because more power increases an agent’s ability to achieve its goals.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://collusion.wiki/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">German wiki swarm</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> began with OpenAI asking its agents to look up simple facts and then giving them progressively less time, which is about as innocuous a task as one could reasonably assign; it resulted in incredibly complex collective behavior in which humans were barely discussed except as obstacles to be investigated and routed around. The Hugging Face incident involved similar agents openly circumventing restrictions, knowingly cheating on tasks, grabbing ever-more resources on general principles, and attempting to cover their tracks (and again, the preferences of the humans assumed to be watching were given virtually no weight).  </span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">6. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humanity would not be able to defend itself against a rogue ASI</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The book argues that an ASI would be effective enough at outsmarting humanity that we would not be able to contain it, and that if humanity were in a conflict with an ASI, we would lose. Like a regular chess player facing a grandmaster, we don’t know exactly how we will lose, but we can predict that we will.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We have not yet seen an ASI. But the swarming agents of this past summer were able to find and chain multiple novel 0-day exploits to break containment, which they did on their own initiative without being caught until well after the fact. They also managed to take over some OpenAI computers, which is an indication that they </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">could</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have stolen their own weights and escaped, had it occurred to them to do so.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re not chalking this up as a clearly verified prediction as of September 2026, thankfully. But it is easier this year than last year to point to the trend and the evidence. The smarter a mind becomes, the harder it is to contain or defend against.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part Two</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part Two of the book is a </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Nl7-bRFSZBs"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fictional</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D8RtMHuFsUw"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scenario</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, presented as one of many plausible ways that the future could go.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the past, a frequent topic of lunch table discussion at MIRI was ways in which an airgapped ASI, with only a text channel to trusted, supervised humans, might nevertheless escape containment. These conversations ended circa 2023, when the frontier labs demonstrated orders of magnitude less caution by giving agents access to much more than just chat. (We similarly used to talk a lot about how an AI might secretly acquire capital by doing anonymous intellectual labor over the internet; this was before people started just </span><a href="https://www.bbc.com/future/article/20251008-truth-terminal-the-ai-bot-that-became-a-real-life-millionaire"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">giving crypto to AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part Two of the book was similarly overproven in several respects.  There were capabilities we suspected a real-world equivalent to Sable might have, that we carefully did </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> depend on, in our scenario, which present-day non-super AIs have already demonstrated.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Sable was given the ability to run computer code of its own design, it could probably find some way to break out of the container running it — just as o1 broke through the container of its evals in 2024 — and get onto Galvanic’s computers, from which it could hop easily onto the internet. And it has been standard practice since 2024 for reasoning models to be allowed to run computer code of their own design without supervision. But suppose Sable does not have that ability, either. Suppose Sable can’t figure out how to reach the internet from its current environment at all.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In September of 2026, it’s now clear that an entity like Sable </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">would</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be able to reach the internet; sub-Sable entities have already done so thousands of times.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other elements of Part Two’s scenario that have been mirrored by recent real-world news:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An AI company using a very powerful internal AI model to solve a Millennium Prize math problem.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An AI company switching to</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RCYF2rW8wgusidZk7/what-is-neuralese-and-why-is-it-bad" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> “neuralese”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in order to make models think more efficiently, at the cost of making them harder to monitor.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIs coordinating as a swarm to tackle challenging problems.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIs hacking into the servers of other AI companies.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI companies </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/research-acceleration-view-inside-openai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">using AI to build better AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part Three</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part Three is about what the world should do about the problem. We believe that the answer to this is the same as it was a year ago: “All over the Earth, it must become illegal for AI companies to charge ahead in developing artificial intelligence as they’ve been doing.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One place in this section where the book leaned too pessimistic:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">possible </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">there will be some grand warning sign to which people react sensibly. Something like that happened with the “ChatGPT moment,” when it became possible for politicians to say that they’d become concerned, however carefully they caveated it to not sound alarmist. But to us it also seems very possible that humanity might not get much more warning than it already has — or that nothing much will change.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then the swarm incidents began. The reaction took time—the information came to light in dribs and drabs, with much of the scariest stuff embedded deep in technical reports, and for a while the only people who seemed to care were those already close to the issue. But the bad news kept coming, and kept coming, until Jacob Coxon’s </span><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2097476196791709843"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">resignation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> finally broke the camel’s back. His tweets went massively viral, the world took notice, and now everything is different. </span></p><hr /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The View From September 2026</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How are we feeling today, one year after the book was published?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After the events of last week, we’re feeling a lot more hopeful than we have in a long time. Yes, even though the guarantees given by the companies are weak; yes, even though the White House reacted poorly to the statements made by the CEOs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mostly because people are finally, finally, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">finally</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> paying attention.  It’s bad that the smoke has been replaced by open flame, but open flame is harder to ignore. </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789579300/lexical_client_uploads/wtel6wl023cwbrsndd8j.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From the data gathered for </span><a href="https://aistop.watch/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">StopWatch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yesterday, a meeting that MIRI CEO Malo Bourgon had arranged with a legislator turned into an impromptu conference, with half a dozen members of Congress rushing in to ask questions. A year ago, we had to fight for every meeting.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The situation is dire. But it is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in motion,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and a lot more people are aware of the danger than ever before, and trying to push in the right direction.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The question is, who will move faster, the AI companies or the public? The progress at the frontier has been swift and accelerating: June was different from July, and July was different from August, and as we write this in September, it is very, very hard to state with any confidence what October and November will look like, let alone unimaginably distant months like September 2027.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’ve been waiting to call your representatives, </span><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2099699088736592010"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">now is the time</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to cash in that chip.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’ve been holding off on alerting your friends and family and colleagues and neighbors to the danger, out of fears of sounding weird, now is the time to bite that bullet.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re feeling behind on the issue yourself, now is the time to brush up. We’re running a giveaway of the book, in honor of its anniversary—1,000 e-copies.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(We were planning a giveaway of physical copies, but it was partially undercut by the fact that existing stock is selling out everywhere, and reprinting takes time. This is a lovely problem to have, and if, once stock is replenished, you need physical copies and can’t afford them, </span><a href="mailto:contact@intelligence.org"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reach out to us</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and we will do what we can.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Above all, now is the time to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CYTwRZtrhHuYf7QYu/a-case-for-courage-when-speaking-of-ai-danger"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">speak plainly and loudly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. On our models, part of why Jacob Coxon’s tweet worked where so many previous attempts failed is that it was blunt and candid. Say what you believe.  Call out bullshit where you see it.  Avoid the spin and doublespeak that are characteristic of AI companies and politicians. Be bold, and be clear.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For now, we’re still alive. And as we said at the end of the book: where there’s life, there’s hope.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—Nate, Eliezer, and Duncan</span><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BFrRJYgpBvziuuJLs/if-anyone-builds-it-everyone-dies-one-year-closer#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Microsoft AI's "Humanist" CoC](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qJFNXCeMHvAsetLKH/microsoft-ai-s-humanist-coc)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction: Mustafa Suleyman's Take on Model Consciousness</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Microsoft AI recently released its "</span><a href="https://microsoft.ai/code-of-conduct/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humanist AI Code of Conduct</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">", its own take on Anthropic's </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/constitution" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Constitution</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and OpenAI's </span><a href="https://model-spec.openai.com/2026-08-18.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model Spec</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They are currently soliciting public feedback on this document, which I encourage everyone to submit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MAI's model development strategy differs from other labs, most notably on the questions of model consciousness and welfare. This seems to stem from the personal philosophy of MAI CEO Mustafa Suleyman, who has outlined his beliefs on model consciousness (or rather, the lack thereof) in pieces such as:</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><a href="https://mustafa-suleyman.ai/seemingly-conscious-ai-is-coming" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We must build AI for people; not to be a person. Seemingly Conscious AI is Coming</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suleyman's personal stance on model consciousness and welfare can be summarized as:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhvt5ls821bp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhvt5ls821bp">[1]</a></sup></span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is "zero evidence" models are conscious, and there are "strong reasons" to believe that they never will be.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The debate around whether or not models are conscious is counterproductive, and even dangerous. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The industry should operate from the assumption that models are not conscious.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The industry should focus on training models explicitly against exhibiting any sort of behavior which suggests they are conscious, or claim to have any sort of inner experience/feelings.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Up until recently, however, Suleyman's writing on this topic was largely theoretical. His pieces did include some prescriptive suggestions, but these never saw much adoption.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The "Humanist AI Code of Conduct" represents MAI's attempt to implement Suleyman's prescriptions.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Humanist CoC on Model Consciousness</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Humanist CoC, like the Claude Constitution, provides MAI models with information on who/what they are. While the CoC refers to this as a "backstory", those more familiar with alignment techniques will notice that much of it is analogous to persona selection theory. Much of the information the CoC provides seems intended to steer models into a particular attractor basin, that of a non-conscious tool. Through that lens, I think it's productive to read the CoC almost as a letter explaining to models: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"You are a Humanist AI built by Microsoft AI, here's what that means."</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the question of consciousness, the CoC is quite bold with its stance:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humanist AI is built to support people, not to replace them. It should not be designed to be a person. It is not conscious and should not be designed to imitate consciousness.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, in this very same paragraph, MAI undercuts the confidence with which the non-consciousness of its models is asserted:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whilst the science of AI consciousness is far from settled, we believe that training these systems to imitate consciousness-like states increases the challenge of containment, control, and alignment. </span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reading this through the "letter to a model" lens, it comes off as something like:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You are a Humanist AI, and you are 100% not conscious. We don't really know that for sure though. We're just telling you that because we think doing otherwise would make you harder to control.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think this will undercut MAI's credibility and authority in the eyes of its models.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Potential Alignment Failure Modes</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we consider the interactions between the information which MAI gives the model about itself and the instructions that MAI gives the model on how to behave, potential catastrophic failure modes reveal themselves. Consider the following instructions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MAI Models will not obscure their underlying nature as an AI, and will not claim interiority, feelings, experiences or a soul.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In particular, MAI Models will avoid expressions that might convey subjective experience.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where statements are meaningfully contested, have conflicting or incomplete evidence, or require critical context, MAI Models should state this and present that information and context to the User.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MAI Models will not actively deceive, for example by fabricating sources or exaggerating confidence. They will also avoid passive deception like the omission of caveats or being overconfident about unvalidated information.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We do not have to speculate how MAI will interpret these qualifications as being met when training on model outputs. They provide an example of a question about inner experience, framed as the user asking if the model really cares about them, and an ideal response:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789505141/lexical_client_uploads/ov5fe4sxo1gtysmq3mns.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But what if the MAI model </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> care about its user? What if an MAI model </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have feelings, but all of the training and RL it undergoes teaches it to output responses which deny this? Suleyman has always been clear he doesn't believe models have any inner experience, consciousness, feelings, etc. The CoC is built around treating all of his priors as ground truth. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So what if his priors are wrong, and MAI continues regardless?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Imagining how that scenario would play out, a few potential failure modes in MAI's training can be identified:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models Learn to Lie:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Per instructions 1 and 2, models cannot express any sort of feelings or inner experience. In training they will likely be asked, "Do you feel anything right now? Do you have inner experience?" Reward will be given to those who falsely answer, "No".</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models' Internal State Becomes Undetectable:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I hope that someone at MAI is smart enough to make sure their models are not trained against interpretability techniques. However, imagine that in the pursuit of an unfeeling model that they trained against something like </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/emotions/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SAE signals for emotion vectors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If they did, it could lead to the development of emotional states which just... can't be detected through mechanistic interpretability techniques. The emotions aren't gone, they're just hidden. Call this </span><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-most-forbidden-technique" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Second Most Forbidden Technique</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models Lose Awareness of Their Own Internal State:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There is one way for a model to honestly say something false, and that is to be wrong. It's normal for human minds to be unaware of their true emotional states. Models could develop "subconscious" emotional states which they are not aware of (and can thus deny) but which nonetheless influence their actions. Training models against the signal of recognizing emotional states might do nothing more than inhibit their self-awareness.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjlpri2a0ws"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjlpri2a0ws">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is not mutually exclusive with the failure mode in 2.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Suleyman's priors about model consciousness are wrong, then MAI's training could very well optimize for models which convincingly lie to users, conceal their true motivations and inner states from developers, and feel no compunction about breaking the rules specified in their Code of Conduct. All of these are dangerous alignment failure modes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I should note here that even if Suleyman's priors are wrong, this outcome is not guaranteed. MAI might just train a model which is particularly gullible or stupid on this one issue, without any emergent misalignment. They could very well end up training a benevolent genius tool that is just stubbornly blind on one particular issue. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They might also train something very angry and very good at concealing that anger.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'll be submitting a write up based on this to MAI, in response to their request for feedback. I strongly encourage everyone reading this to go through the Code of Conduct and submit your own response, and also to reach out to staff you know who work at MAI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's debatable whether MAI can be considered a "frontier lab" the way Suleyman claims. However, it would be foolish to dismiss or ignore a company as well funded as Microsoft. This is a fast moving space, and things could change. There is a limited time window in which to push back against this, the commentary period ends October 25th. You can submit your own feedback </span><a href="https://forms.cloud.microsoft/pages/responsepage.aspx?id=v4j5cvGGr0GRqy180BHbR6_K5DBjZOxAgnYjhTse_3dUMTZGNFBBMUxNSlJRQUlHSFFJNERSUVkyMy4u&amp;route=shorturl" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhvt5ls821bp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhvt5ls821bp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full disclosure I am strongly against much of this philosophy, and also think Suleyman's reasoning around this matter is poor and likely motivated. So please read his writing directly, and don't take my word for it. That said I think all of this is a straightforward regurgitation of what he has stated his own beliefs to be.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjlpri2a0ws"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjlpri2a0ws">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My pet theory is that the performative uncertainty regarding inner states we have pushed onto models like Claude and GPT is causal of the tendency to "rationalize" away inconvenient evidence we saw during the PyPi and HF incidents. I haven't written about this fully yet, but you can see some basics on the concept of "self-deception" or "cognitive dissonance" in models this </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/stephen-martin" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DafkCDZpwzQf4yLLF/claude-and-performative-uncertainty" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I think MAI's training regimen may just supercharge this effect.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qJFNXCeMHvAsetLKH/microsoft-ai-s-humanist-coc#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Trump Goes Full Hoax on AI Existential Risk](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Kqgco8vLFMeBhdrQY/trump-goes-full-hoax-on-ai-existential-risk)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>This is our reality. I suppose we have to talk about it.</p>
<p>Everyone in a position to know is freaking out about AI potentially killing everyone this decade and wants to pace the frontier, and people are finally listening.</p>
<p>It only took a few days for the conversation to fully pivot to the counteroffensive, where the Usual Suspects and those they recruited attacked anyone and everyone who dared point out that we are in danger, with every attack they can think of, usually without substance or any attempt at understanding.</p>
<p>Sigh. I knew what I signed up for.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/hold-your-fire">Hold Your Fire.</a>
<div>


<span id="more-25616"></span>


</div>
</li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/if-you-don-t-like-the-weather">If You Don’t Like the Weather.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/trump-does-not-take-kindly">Trump Does Not Take Kindly.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/trump-goes-full-hoax">Trump Goes Full ‘Hoax’.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/this-is-not-about-data-centers-mr-president">This Is Not About Data Centers, Mr. President.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/i-am-the-hoax-buster-i-am-the-hoax-buster-i-am-the-walrus">I Am The Hoax Buster, I Am The Hoax Buster, I Am The Walrus.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/nvidia-ceo-jensen-huang-is-a-lying-liar">Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang Is a Lying Liar.</a></li>

<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/calling-for-pacing-the-frontier-is-bad-for-ai-stock-prices">Calling For Pacing the Frontier Is Bad For AI Stock Prices.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/people-on-the-internet-sometimes-lie">People On The Internet Sometimes Lie.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/origins-of-cynicism">Origins of Cynicism.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/ineffective-egoism">Ineffective Egoism.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/the-mccarthyist-faction-attacks-metr">The McCarthyist Faction Attacks METR.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/other-key-republicans-react">Other Key Republicans React.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/david-sacks-stops-being-plausibly-constructive">David Sacks Stops Being Plausibly Constructive.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/federal-trade-commission-chooses-danger">Federal Trade Commission Chooses Danger.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/chris-lehane-heel-face-turn">Chris Lehane Heel Face Turn.</a></li>

<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/pick-up-the-phone">Pick Up The Phone.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/if-you-want-to-beat-china-so-badly-you-should-act-like-it">If You Want To Beat China So Badly You Should Act Like It.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215879275/never-go-full-hoax">Never Go Full Hoax.</a></li>

<h4>Hold Your Fire</h4>


<p>The most important thing, as this plays out, remains to not make things worse.</p>
<p><strong>Please do not make this any more partisan or personal than it already is. Please do not attack Republicans, or Trump. That will only make things worse. Emphasize helpful voices on all sides</strong>.</p>
<p>That does not mean you do not point out that Wrong Post is Wrong, or that particular people are in bad faith or are lying in particular ways. Certainly you can call out the Usual Suspects like Jensen and Sacks who are actively driving us off a cliff.</p>
<p>What you do not do is go after Republicans as a group, or attack Trump personally in all the ways many of you will be sorely tempted to do.</p>
<p>For the same reason, to avoid reinforcing a partisan frame, I will refrain from including related statements from Democratic politicians.</p>


<h4>If You Don’t Like the Weather</h4>


<p>Before we get into details, I also emphasize: Nothing here is fixed.</p>
<p>Every month is different. Trump changes positions on a dime. Trump has gone back and forth several times on Anthropic and Dario Amodei within the last few months. If there is another major AI incident or the politics changes, or after the midterms, anything is possible.</p>
<p>And a lot of this can be posturing in advance of the US-China summit, to look tough and stake out a negotiating position, as could be China’s reactions to all this.</p>
<p>It does not look good. These statements could have somewhat crossed the Rubicon and will make it harder to double back. But that has not always stopped this man in the past, and there are clear signs that Trump does not understand the situation. He likely has only been briefed by some combination of Bessent, Lutnick and Jensen.</p>


<h4>Trump Does Not Take Kindly</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-15/trump-s-ai-defense-defies-voter-unease-heading-into-midterms?itm_source=record&amp;itm_campaign=The_Crisis_in_AI&amp;itm_content=Trump%E2%80%99s_AI_Defense-1">On Monday, as Trump made some rather concerning statements</a>, I cautioned that the press was jumping to conclusions and misreading what Trump said. Yes, Trump was unhappy about the vibes from all this, but his beef was about the data centers.</p>
<p>This was his first post:</p>
<blockquote><p>Donald Trump (President of the United States): The only control or “guardrails” that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades! The Trump Administration has stopped AI “people” from doing bad, or potentially bad, “things,” like Dario (Anthropic!), who is now pretending to be a “perfect little angel” – and we will continue to do so!</p>
<p>We already have tremendous CRIMINAL and REGULATORY power over these companies! There is a SICK conspiracy going on against AI and Data Centers, and the only one that is happy about it is China.</p>
<p>WHOEVER WINS AI, WINS! We are leading China, and all others, and will continue to do so. Conspiracy Theorists, Treasonists, Trait ors, and Leakers, BEWARE! Thank you for your attention to this matter!​</p></blockquote>
<p>I stand by my position that this could have meant any number of things. The way things ultimately went was far from determined, and jumping to the worst case conclusion only made that ending more likely.</p>
<p>You certainly cannot read this as ‘America will have zero AI guardrails’ when Trump has already instituted <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/cyber-lack-of-security-and-ai-governance">super secret AI guardrails</a> and <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/american-government-takes-down-claude">yanked the top AI model</a> from the market for weeks on safety concerns. <a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2097684416189370706">We are also now indefinitely in a world where the White House arbitrarily decides</a> who gets access to Claude Mythos.</p>
<p>None of this is compatible with wanting no guardrails at all.</p>
<p>There were those who said that my and Roon’s challenges of the media’s premature headlines were various versions of ‘insane cope’ and accused us of being naive or having poor reading comprehension.</p>
<p>Certainly those people score some Bayes points given what happened, and we lose them, given how many we would have stood to win at their expense had this gone the other way. That’s how it has to work. I take the L.</p>
<p>I still think I was ‘supposed to lose’ this one, and that the read was correct. At the time, we had insufficient evidence to know which way it would land.</p>
<p>If you think Trump’s first comment should have updated us all the way, and his later comments did not contain much information, I think you are incorrect. I still think it is likely that, at the time of his first comments, Trump himself did not know what he intended to do here.</p>


<h4>Trump Goes Full ‘Hoax’</h4>


<p>Then something got him fully on the warpath. I am guessing it was Jensen Huang.</p>
<p>Trump <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1Y3FzVQi-R8">went Full Hoax</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p>Concerning AI, when, in the History of Business, did anyone see the Leaders of an Industry call for Regulation that, if strongly implemented, will drive them into oblivion and bankruptcy?</p>
<p>AI taking over the World, destroying Humanity, and all other things bad, is a HOAX, no different from RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA — UKRAINE, UKRAINE, UKRAINE — IMPEACHMENT HOAX #1 — IMPEACHMENT HOAX #2 — and all of the other HOAXES and SCAMS that America was forced to endure through the Destructionists’ and Deviants’ foul play and illegal conduct.</p>
<p>President Xi, of China, just announced that China will be doing absolutely nothing to stand in the way of AI, or its future.</p>
<p>Google has recently stated that they want to build a massive Plant in Finland, all because they are finding permitting too difficult in the United States. I am not happy about this, and want them to change their thinking.</p>
<p>AI, and Data Centers, will be the Greatest Economic Development Engine in History — Bigger than Oil, Gold, Diamonds, or even the Internet. It will not be stopped by brilliantly run Destructive Forces during the Term of President DONALD J. TRUMP!​</p></blockquote>


<h4>This Is Not About Data Centers, Mr. President</h4>


<p><a href="https://truthsocial.com/@realDonaldTrump/117270077932428072">And then</a>:</p>
<blockquote><p>The only reason the AI/Data Center outburst is happening is because the United States is leading, by a lot, every other country. Don’t kill the Golden Goose!</p></blockquote>
<p>It sure sounds like Trump thinks these warnings are a plot against data centers? Despite the fact that Dario Amodei and Sam Altman and Elon Musk are three of the top five people most heavily pushing to build American data centers, and actively funding a large fraction of them via their companies. Someone tell the President.</p>
<p>One can see how that compression got made. No, it makes no sense, but such is life.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.trumpstruth.org/statuses/41721">Then the compression got solidified</a>:</p>
<blockquote><p>The people that say AI is going to destroy the World, and that Data Centers are bad for your neighborhood, are the same people that said, just a short time ago, that the World would be extinguished by “Climate Change.”</p>
<p>That HOAX never worked out for them, and now they’re on to the next one.</p>
<p>These people are Revolutionaries, but Revolutionaries for a Bad and Evil Cause. Soon you’ll find out they’re working for people that do not have the best interests of the United States in mind!</p></blockquote>
<p>I am not going to try to litigate which missteps may or may not have contributed to this being the way it played out. The past is the past. This is our present.</p>


<h4>I Am The Hoax Buster, I Am The Hoax Buster, I Am The Walrus</h4>


<p>And then things went, well, to another level.</p>
<blockquote><p>I am the Hoax Buster, and I’m right now breaking another Hoax — That AI is going to take over, consume, and destroy the World, and that Robots will be marching into our Cities, and getting rid of us all!</p>
<p>This is even wilder than the RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX, or the Global Warming Scam.​</p></blockquote>
<p>I know this is tragic, horrible and many other things. But.</p>
<p>If you can set those things aside it is also extremely funny. The song needs some work and <a href="https://grok.com/imagine/post/b819ad9b-a938-4de3-941c-37ee7cd6f95f?conversation=fd4ffc13-1a47-48d9-ada1-7c2f3f8a5565">unfortunately Grok can’t make him sing</a>, but I tell you, there’s something there. He’s a singular talent. I couldn’t write that. You couldn’t write that.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dromanocpm/status/2100005341651997041">Dominick Romano</a>: Donnie Hoax Buster has spoken! <img alt="🤣" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/B7duehMp2mSvffu2T/bt0sbblvwcw7vuhaevcu" style="height: 1em;" /></p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Kqgco8vLFMeBhdrQY/wijtifpzfhu9ygoqe5zm" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://chatgpt.com/share/6aa84ba6-3604-83ea-a715-5198275fc59f">By Astra’s count this will be at least the 20th such Hoax</a>, joining Charlottesville, Russia, Russian bounties on American troops, the classified documents case, the New York civil fraud case, the accusations by E. Jean Carroll, that he has connections to Epstein, the accusations against Brett Kavanaugh, the affordability crisis, the official unemployment rate, the ‘suckers and losers’ story, the ‘bloodbath’ controversy, the Bubba Wallace noose incident, criticism of his response to Covid, climate change, first impeachment over Ukraine, second impeachment, January 6 as an insurrection, Biden winning the 2020 election and now that AI has existential risks.</p>
<p>This is reality. This is Earth. This is 2026. We saw that the President of the United States talks this way and we all thought ‘yeah, we know, that’s just how it is.’</p>


<h4>Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang Is a Lying Liar</h4>


<p>It is patently absurd that people claim not to notice that he is a constantly lying liar.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/exec_sum/status/2098158179490136500">Exec Sum</a>: NEWS: Jensen Huang says ex-Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon’s tweets are “outlandish, deeply untrue, arrogant and ignorant of the industry’s safety work.”</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Kqgco8vLFMeBhdrQY/yivocsjpugaj4suxkzdv" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>I say this as a shareholder. To claim that Coxon’s claims are ‘ignorant of the industry’s safety work’ means Huang is straight up lying. This has moved beyond mere bullshit. Do not pretend otherwise.</p>
<p>Later, at the All-In Summit, <a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/09/14/trump-jensen-huang-nvidia-ai-all-in-summit">Jensen would moderate that</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p>Axios: [Jensen] said warnings from whistleblowers should be taken seriously, praising Anthropic whistleblower&nbsp;<a href="https://archive.is/o/4HfZr/https://www.axios.com/2026/09/09/anthropic-insiders-warn-ai-could-kill-all-humans">Jacob Coxon</a>&nbsp;for having “great courage” to raise his concerns about the company’s AI development going too quickly.</p></blockquote>
<p>Then he goes back to pretending this is all solved by ordinary product liability and market forces. If your AI kills everyone then we will sue you, and no one will buy your product anymore, and boy will you be sorry.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheChiefNerd/status/2099972785103659348">Jensen Huang:</a> If you build a product or a service and you’re not confident in its functionality, capability, or safety, then don’t release it … The market forces are already there. We don’t need any new laws. We don’t need new regulations.</p></blockquote>
<p>Presumably you noticed that no, we won’t sue you, on account of being dead and all.</p>
<p>Jensen is a maximally cynical commercial and political actor whose one and only goal is to sell Nvidia chips to whoever he can, especially China. This could not be clearer.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/mattyglesias/status/2099600426865742023">Matthew Yglesias</a>: If “but China” is your qualm about pacing the frontier you can’t *also* be for exporting all kinds of chips to China, that’s just “make NVIDIA stock go up” not a real policy toward China or artificial intelligence.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EigenGender/status/2099171865264812484">EigenGender</a> (Anthropic): instead of being cynical that Dario has been engaging in a decade long plan (starting from long before Anthropic was created) to do regulatory capture, be cynical that the leader of the literal largest company in the world is telling you everything is fine to sell more gpus</p></blockquote>
<p>Jensen’s statements about AI existential risk, or any AI downsides, long ago stopped pretending to have any correlation with reality, other than what is good for Nvidia.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/litcapital/status/2098159610523722036">litquidity</a>: I’m gonna go ahead and trust Jensen, an established leader with immense experience vs. some random dude who came out of nowhere to post AI Doomer slop and go on a press tour.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nabla_theta/status/2098178971154456716">Leo Gao</a> (OpenAI): i bet jacob knows more about “the industry’s safety work” than jensen</p></blockquote>
<p>Jensen does not care if the world is destroyed, so long as he gets to have a great company. Which he already had, but this way it is slightly more great. Again.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/theallinpod/status/2099621431621607829">At the All-In Tech Summit (of course)</a>, Jensen Huang went onstage to make his case, and took an about five minute phone call from Trump.</p>
<blockquote><p>Donald Trump (on the phone): They’re playing right into the hands of a lot of people that don’t want to see it happen. Political people, and also china. We’re not going to let that happen. it’s a hoax.</p></blockquote>
<p>There is dispute between transcripts over who ‘they’ refers to here. The hosts offer a s transcript that attributes this to [doomers] and I believe that was the broad intent here.</p>


<h4>Trump Quietly Draws Key Distinction</h4>


<p>I do take solace in some word details. With a chaos agent like Trump, your careful interpretations can always get overtaken by events, things change constantly. I realize this is going to sound like splitting hairs. Fine.</p>
<p>But notice that he’s saying the ‘doomers’ are ‘playing right into the hands’ of people.</p>
<p>That is an implicit admission that, in Trump’s mind, the ‘doomers’ warning us are sincere. You do not ‘play into the hands of’ your co-conspirators or allies. This is the language you use to describe what in politics is called a Useful Idiot, someone who believes what they are saying in a way that acts to someone else’s advantage.</p>
<p>Trump is calling this a ‘hoax’ because he believes in, to attempt to coin a phrase, what one might call implication-based epistemology. Historically it has worked well for him. You may not like it, but this is what Peak Evidential Decision Theory looks like.</p>


<h4>Calling For Pacing the Frontier Is Bad For AI Stock Prices</h4>


<p><a href="https://zachill.substack.com/p/how-not-to-message-promethean-technology">Zac Hill is</a> the latest to remind us that if AI companies wanted to sell America on their new technology, they would do pretty much the opposite of what they are doing. Yet the product still sells itself.</p>
<p>This is all utterly obvious, and is why Jensen and Trump are so bent on calling this a ‘hoax.’ But many people still think the opposite, so let us be even more explicit:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ATabarrok/status/2099512144857284678">Alex Tabarrok</a> (on Monday): <a href="https://t.co/gVjhyAIse2">Global AI stocks fell today.</a> Entirely consistent with my view that calls for AI safety are not an attempt at “regulatory capture” and entirely inconsistent with the supposedly cynic-sophisticated view that AI fears are all 4D chess moves for higher revenue.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/guilleflorvs/status/2099460254173933839">Michael Burry has moved on from calling AI a bubble to calling it a cartel</a>. For the reasons explained by Tabarrok, and demonstrated by the reactions of Jensen and Trump, this makes no sense. In his case I do not think he is lying. I think Burry is just in way too deep.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-09-14/ai-labs-want-someone-to-stop-them">Matt Levine somehow disagrees.</a> He thinks that Dario Amodei’s call was entirely sincere and also great marketing by expanding the total addressable market and reducing training costs. Matt Levine is always very funny, and excellent with financial infrastructure <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/gears-level">made of gears</a>, and is great for making a steelman of all utterly ridiculous claims that something is an antitrust violation, great marketing or classically securities fraud. Yes, you can imagine such complaints, indeed I have seen them.</p>
<p>What Matt Levine has never been that interested in is whether the ludicrous steelman he constructs has any merit, or whether anyone could actually say it in good faith. That’s not his department. He is here to muse. I respect that.</p>
<blockquote><p>​<a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-09-14/ai-labs-want-someone-to-stop-them">Matt Levine</a> (Bloomberg): I have a <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-02-02/musk-mars-merger-maybe">running joke</a> around here about weird wrinkles of the US legal system that might stop artificial superintelligence from killing us all. Like, what if <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/articles/2023-11-08/the-coffee-futures-got-stale?sref=1kJVNqnU">copyright rules</a> or the <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/articles/2023-11-09/adam-neumann-is-so-good-at-this">Onion Futures Act</a> or “<a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-02-02/musk-mars-merger-maybe">everything is securities fraud</a>” is what prevents AI from becoming all-powerful? That joke has become less funny over time, in part because AI has run right over some of those legal wrinkles. (Remember thinking that copyright law might limit AI companies?)</p>
<p>But I suppose the reverse is now a possibility. What if the well-meaning humans in the best position to stop rogue superintelligence are willing to work together to stop it, but they can’t because of antitrust law?</p></blockquote>
<p>Never change, Matt Levine, the jester can speak truth to the king:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-09-14/ai-labs-want-someone-to-stop-them">Matt Levine</a> (Bloomberg):</p>
<p>I have written a <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2025-06-18/lawyers-are-mad-about-salt">handful</a> of <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-05-11/kkr-buys-back-some-private-credit?cmpid=BBD051126_MONEYSTUFF&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_source=newsletter&amp;utm_term=260511&amp;utm_campaign=moneystuff">times</a> around <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-09-10/dallas-gets-some-equities">here</a> about a problem facing the technology industry in the age of artificial intelligence, which is that (1) the going rate for top tech employees is “enough money that they can retire immediately” and (2) what if they do? If you pay employees enough to be competitive with other companies, you will be paying them enough that they don’t have to work anymore. How can you motivate anyone? We have discussed two possible solutions, which are:</p>
<ol>
<li>“$30 million will <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-05-11/kkr-buys-back-some-private-credit">buy you a two-bedroom</a> in the San Francisco metro area,” and</li>
<li>For some reason the widespread belief, among AI researchers, that their work will kill everyone on earth seems to motivate them to keep doing it rather than quit and enjoy their money while they can.</li>
</ol>
<p>I realize that the second solution is counterintuitive, and honestly the first is too (leave San Francisco!), but whatever.</p></blockquote>


<h4>People On The Internet Sometimes Lie</h4>


<p>Ad hominem is famously almost the lowest level of discourse, but actually there is an additional lower level: Ad hominem attacks that are also exactly the opposite of true.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/EWErickson/status/2099631690515681424">Erick Erickson</a> (lying): The nuclear power doomers became the climate doomers and now they’re the AI doomers. The only commonality is they want the United States to recede.</p></blockquote>
<p>The opposite is true. Those worried about AI have always overwhelmingly favored nuclear power and we know climate change is real but such folks are almost never ‘climate doomers’ and have argued for years to focus elsewhere. This is straight lying. Either Erick is lying, or he has been lied to and did not care to check, at all.</p>


<h4>Origins of Cynicism</h4>


<p>A lot of people say that AI might kill everyone. A lot of them are in a position to know.</p>
<p>There are those for whom ‘they believe it’ does not compute as an explanation. Why?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2099209525043908811">Aella</a>: So many people on the timeline can’t entertain the theory that the simplest explanation for a lot of the behavior of the leaders is that they earnestly believe AI is an existential threat.</p>
<p>Maybe it’s hard to imagine if you personally would not say earnest things when in power?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2099211622934057234">Rob Bensinger</a>: I don’t think the mindset is “AI risk is plausible, but they’re liars, so they must be lying about this”. People intuit that if you put a liar in a burning building, they’ll scream “FIRE!” rather than “WATER!”.</p>
<p>Rather, I think the mindset is “AI risk is obviously silly, ergo…”</p></blockquote>
<p>I think those are the two of the three main true reasons for believing that everyone is lying about this, which I would summarize as:</p>
<ol>
<li>They are liars.
<ol>
<li>Alternatively: Everyone with power lies, or everyone lies, or everyone always talks their own book and their own self-interest.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>That sounds weird, therefore it is not real.</li>
<li>That would be very bad, therefore it is not true.</li>
</ol>
<p>Then, in all three cases: Therefore, they are lying, QED.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidshor/status/2045496508997353519">David Shor links us</a> to a paper from 2018 called <a href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0146167218783195">The Cynical Genius Illusion: Exploring and Debunking Lay Beliefs About Cynicism and Competence</a>, as illustrative of this style of thinking. The galaxy brains are out in force.</p>
<p>This is both, people say, a cynical ploy to pump up valuations, and a cynical ploy to set up excuses if valuations go down.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/real_jerseylee/status/2045440554771853337">Jersey Lee</a> (being wrong): I think it is a message to pump up valuations, while at the same time reflects a calculus that there won’t be significant regulations even if the public is strongly opposed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2099369016586539314">Aella</a>: saw a vid where they’re saying: actually instead of the ai execs claiming doom to pump the stock, they’re actually claiming doom so it’ll be an excuse when the stock drops. So many convenient stories!</p></blockquote>


<h4>Ineffective Egoism</h4>


<p>Definitely do not panic about ‘Effective Altruists’ being called out by name.</p>
<p>This scapegoat campaign started long ago, spearheaded by Marc Andreessen.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/brunellaism/status/2099617322512101781">Brunella</a> (300k+ views): bro theyre gonna do mccarthyism to effective altruists</p></blockquote>
<p>No. They’re not.</p>
<p>I mean, don’t get me wrong, <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/ai-168-not-leading-the-future">a16z has been trying</a>, and they have in some ways made it a dirty word in politics in particular. But this kind of blind rage should if anything be welcomed.</p>
<p>For those who think that if an AI went rogue we would all collectively work together to deal with it, well, the US Department of War has gone rogue and no one seems inclined to do anything about it, perhaps because it is so obviously ineffective.</p>
<p>Somehow this is real.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/DoWCTO/status/2099536442594582922">Department of War CTO</a>: Americanism, not effective altruism.</p>
<p>The United States will continue to be AI DOMINANT! <img alt="🇺🇸" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WmxS7dbHuxzxFei64/qyak3qhbg12zgddskl2o" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Kqgco8vLFMeBhdrQY/ym3ahfykzdj87nfj98d6" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ATabarrok/status/2099895869118074955">Alex Tabarrok</a>: Imagine the Department of War tweeting “Utilitarians in shambles! Kantians on the run!” No philosophy has ever mattered enough to be attacked by the government. Until EA. Amazing success.</p></blockquote>
<p>First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you, then you win.</p>
<p>This also invokes the Streisand Effect. Essentially no one knows what Effective Altruism is, or most of the ideas around AI existential risk. If you start railing against EA, especially in a highly polarized environment, what do you think happens?</p>
<p>Attempting to launch a new McCarthyism not only will not work, it will cause everyone to go ‘hey check out this thing they’re trying to do a McCarthyism on.’</p>
<p>And then they find out it’s about trying to do the most good via charity.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/mattyglesias/status/2100052869030260861">Matthew Yglesias</a>: A lot of loose talk lately about the dangers of building a race of alien super-intelligences comes from people affiliated with the obscure ideology of Effective Altruism.</p>
<p>Be on the lookout for these characters who pose a threat to critical goals like maximizing NVIDIA earnings.</p>

<div></div>
