</blockquote>


<h4>The McCarthyist Faction Attacks METR</h4>


<p>This is a logical first target. Maybe I should be more concerned but mostly I find it counterproductive and more importantly hilarious.</p>
<p>This is the actual, real life front page of the New York Post, enshrining this story alongside past winners like ‘Headless Body in Topless Bar’:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Kqgco8vLFMeBhdrQY/ftzhpfjmtl572gfyrsw2" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Who wouldn’t want to be the ‘Wizards of AI’?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nypost/status/2099992081624096899">New York Post</a>: <a href="https://trib.al/IuA3uHW">Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei’s handpicked AI watchdog has deep ties to woke Effective Altruism movement</a>: ‘a complete joke’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/vikhyatk/status/2100012188224327966">vik</a>: concerning</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Kqgco8vLFMeBhdrQY/mr6b4t85safzzahevxvr" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://nypost.com/2026/09/15/business/anthropic-ceo-dario-amodeis-handpicked-ai-watchdog-has-deep-ties-to-effective-altruism-movement-a-complete-joke/">Thomas Barrabi</a> (New York Post): The would-be John Connors at METR purport to be fully independent, but the group traces its roots directly back to key figures in Effective Altruism, whose Silicon Valley-based devotees once included disgraced crypto boss Sam Bankman-Fried, one of Anthropic’s biggest early investors.</p></blockquote>
<p>If there was a new version of Pangram that used AI to detect Hack Job Hit Piece Voice, you would not need it, because this is so classic. You see, [A] claims to be independent of [B], but [A] traces its roots back to [C], which once associated with [D], and [D] was a bad, bad person, who once was associated with [B], so checkmate [outgroup], we have discovered your conspiracy.</p>
<p>It continues in this vein throughout. You see, there are (gasp) Effective Altruists at METR, and some of the same people who invested in Anthropic also helped fund METR, like Jaan Tallinn, it must be some grand conspiracy. And so on. There are zero new facts, or even anything that is not common knowledge among those paying attention.</p>
<p>The ‘complete joke’ line was obtained from Perry Metzger, who goes around posting unhinged rants in reply to various Twitter posts including my own, and is chairman of the industry anti-regulation group Alliance for the Future. That offers a guess as to the origins of Barrabi’s hit piece.</p>
<p>So does this, which could be either a deep confusion at the White House, where they have been known to make such conflations, or the source making things up.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://nypost.com/2026/09/15/business/anthropic-ceo-dario-amodeis-handpicked-ai-watchdog-has-deep-ties-to-effective-altruism-movement-a-complete-joke/">Thomas Barrabi</a> (New York Post): “METR is essentially Anthropic in another form,” a source familiar with the White House’s thinking told The Post on Tuesday.​</p></blockquote>
<p>The ‘woke’ label is purely something they threw in the headline and first sentence, with neither any actual truth to it nor any evidence provided, as per usual. METR is not, in any sense whatsoever, ‘woke.’</p>
<p>The ‘globalist’ label in the headline is a complete non-sequitur. Usual playbook.</p>
<p>The other implicit argument is, look at these large photos of so many prominent METR employees who, unlike Dario Amodei, photograph well and look good.</p>
<p>That is not to say that there are no issues with potential conflicts of interest. Yes, of course we also will want auditors that bring additional perspectives, to work alongside METR, both at Anthropic and elsewhere. If you want the ‘fair play’ outside view hit piece, <a href="https://www.verysane.ai/p/is-metr-a-meaningful-check-on-anthropic">see SE Gyges</a>.</p>
<p>The issues that are real here are well known, acknowledged, and were not avoidable. You need former lab employees and those whose backgrounds have links to the labs, because no one else has the technical expertise to do the job. And you need auditors to get paid enough to attract strong talent and not charge for their services, which means you need to hit up the philanthropists who care about such issues. Most industries have some form of exactly this problem.</p>
<p>It would be really great, you would totally own the libs, if you were to spin up your own outside view highly credible and technically competent monitoring organizations to compete in this space. My lord, I hope no one does that, we definitely don’t need a lot more such efforts. Would be a real shame.</p>
<p>It would be even worse for Anthropic and METR if you were to say that in order to generate a real check on Anthropic you need the audits and evaluators to be universal, mandatory and protected by law, and otherwise given more teeth to be a ‘real check.’</p>
<p>For additional fun color, this was the reporter’s previous hack hit job effort, which some say was even funnier but was tragically denied the front page:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2100065959944024449">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): same reporter [Thomas Barrabi] who did that nasty hit piece on caleb watney btw</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/albrgr/status/2100069024755556553">Alexander Berger</a>: hey they’re going to be selling a lot of copies</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2100087703425712311">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): New York post analytics staff befuddled by sudden uptick in delivery volume to this one address on telegraph ave</p></blockquote>
<blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/hLn3SakowZLFWobHf/zguczic1aqoq8yqgjkyi" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/hLn3SakowZLFWobHf/osyxlugf9zhvczwtr909" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>To explain: This was an Obvious Nonsense hit piece against Dean Ball full of clear untruths, and also the insert picture that was supposed to be Dean Ball was for some reason instead Caleb Watney. <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/210075321/the-lighter-side">See here for my full coverage</a> from AI #181.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2100113579240485193">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): nobody should patronize those who newly have opinions about third party evaluators. non-experts forming opinions fast is part of the american tradition. the constitution itself was, in a very important sense, vibe coded by 20 somethings with concepts of a plan.</p>
<p>but newbies being fleeced is also part of the american tradition. “caveat emptor” is a provision of a contract signed by an adult, not some god-given right to be treated as something other than a little child. you will be treated as a child no matter what.</p>
<p>you are the buyer. it is your job to beware.</p></blockquote>
<p>There are indeed all the signs of a coordinated smear campaign against METR to make them sound bad to right-wing echo chambers. The next few weeks are likely to suck in many ways for the METR folks, especially as some rather vicious people dig around for the tiniest bit of actual dirt. I do not expect this to work, indeed I expect it to backfire via the Streisand Effect.</p>
<p>Laugh it off as best you can. Stay strong. Hang it in the Louvre.</p>


<h4>Other Key Republicans React</h4>


<p>There are also many Republicans, with the mainstream arm of that led by Ron DeSantis but also increasingly many others, who increasingly understand the stakes or simply understand the politics.</p>
<p>The Usual Suspects may have Trump currently talking about a hoax, but this remains a bipartisan moment, and many are not going to fall in line. <a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/09/15/ai-congress-regulation-bill-trump-bipartisan?utm_campaign=mrf-utm_campaign=editorial&amp;utm_source=x&amp;utm_medium=owned_social&amp;utm_source=twitter&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;mrfcid=202609156a9e36c1f046393007a41316">Even now the issue often remains remarkably bipartisan</a>, and not only when you see Steve Bannon on stage with Bernie Sanders.</p>
<p>Axios’s post is largely about the bipartisan investigation of the HuggingFace attacks where Senator Hawley is taking point, but it also includes the more recent concerns.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Kqgco8vLFMeBhdrQY/ncgmaqffypvldos7s8re" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/09/15/ai-congress-regulation-bill-trump-bipartisan">Andrew Solender</a>: Lawmakers who <a href="https://archive.is/o/2aoB3/https://www.axios.com/2026/09/11/congress-ai-anthropic-coxon-researcher-democrats">want to regulate AI</a> are doing something unusual as Congress barrels towards a lengthy recess with no tangible solution in sight: They’re working across the aisle.</p>
<p>… The latest example: Sens. Josh Hawley (R-Mo.) and Richard Blumenthal (D-Conn.) are pressing congressional leadership for a vote on&nbsp;<a href="https://archive.is/o/2aoB3/https://www.congress.gov/119/bills/s2938/BILLS-119s2938is.pdf">their bill</a>&nbsp;to establish a federal program to assess and monitor AI systems.</p>
<p>… <strong>State of play: </strong>Several new bipartisan efforts to regulate AI have cropped up in the week since several current and former employees at Anthropic and OpenAI went public with concerns about the existential risks posed by their own products.</p>
<ul>
<li>Reps. Russell Fry (R-S.C.) and Suhas Subramanyam (D-Va.) are founding a caucus focused on AI small businesses, called the Innovators Caucus, as Axios <a href="https://archive.is/o/2aoB3/https://www.axios.com/2026/09/14/house-republicans-democrats-ai-caucus-congress">reported</a> Monday.</li>
<li>Among the group’s stated goals is starting work on a federal framework to regulate AI, as well as bringing small businesses to the AI policymaking table.</li>
</ul>
</blockquote>
<p>House Speaker Mike Johnson continues to act sanely and this continues to be reported strangely. First Politico tried to make it sound like Johnson wanted to fully pass the buck to the companies, and now Bloomberg says he ‘risks Trump’s wrath’ over his very milquetoast statements:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-15/trump-s-ai-defense-defies-voter-unease-heading-into-midterms?itm_source=record&amp;itm_campaign=The_Crisis_in_AI&amp;itm_content=Trump%E2%80%99s_AI_Defense-1">Michael Shepard, Erik Wasson, and Hadriana Lowenkron</a> (Bloomberg): Risking Trump’s wrath, Republican House Speaker Mike Johnson cautiously signaled his openness to regulation, saying on Monday that lawmakers “potentially” will need to impose guardrails on the industry, though he cautioned it would likely take “some time” to fashion legislation with broad enough support to pass.</p>
<p>“This is a very complex issue that requires a lot of work and a bipartisan solution,” Johnson told reporters at the Capitol.</p></blockquote>
<p>Indeed, Johnson said Monday that there will be a White House meeting with AI executives by early next week at the latest, prior to the China summit.</p>
<p>Even now, Trump is being misinterpreted. Of course Trump endorses the need for guardrails. Trump said on Sunday on Air Force One, to reporters, that ‘we can put guardrails.’ Trump is saying that there is no need to worry because he is such a great President that will ensure the right guardrails, not that we don’t need any at all.</p>
<p>Again, he has a <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-ai-ad-hoc-prior-restraint-era">super secret prior restraint testing regime </a>already in place and his administration yanked Fable 5 from the market in a panic. There is no ‘wrath’ here.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SenRickScott/status/2099557655127756985">Republican Senator Rick Scott says the AI companies</a> <a href="https://www.foxnews.com/live-news/artificial-intelligence-openai-sam-altman-09-14-26">have said what they are building could kill mankind</a>, so pretty obvious they need to slow down and get their house in order so we can move forward safely, with America leading the way or else China will lead, which will hurt, especially for our kids, whose safety our good corporate citizens must protect online.</p>
<p>I’ve read this three times and I still can’t figure out to what extent he understands the situation. Which is progressively making it funnier.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RonDeSantis/status/2097754478585720919">Ron DeSantis continues to call for channeling our technological innovations</a> rather than letting the AI labs do whatever they want.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GovCox/status/2099586614431531290">Republican Utah Governor Spencer Cox calls for Congress</a> to establish a consistent national framework for AI, requiring incident reporting, protecting whistleblowers, ensuring independent evaluation and imposing strict export controls.</p>
<p>(The Tweet is by AI but the full statement is human written.)</p>


<h4>David Sacks Stops Being Plausibly Constructive</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/atrupar/status/2099497586889495013">JD Vance finds the whole thing ‘a little weird</a>’ with companies asking for regulation, saying it feels ‘a little bit to me like a Trojan horse.’</p>
<p>David Sacks was initially hostile but in a potentially constructive way. He has now retreated to joining the other Usual Suspects like his co-host Jason and is back to fully lying about what is being requested, as per usual. You can say ‘let’s impose restrictions on only the frontier’ and the Usual Suspects will flat out claim you said the opposite, over and over, every time, no matter what.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-15/trump-s-ai-defense-defies-voter-unease-heading-into-midterms?itm_source=record&amp;itm_campaign=The_Crisis_in_AI&amp;itm_content=Trump%E2%80%99s_AI_Defense-1">David Sacks</a>: I don’t really believe this claim that they can’t make their products safe unless the government steps in and imposes requirements on companies that aren’t even at the frontier​.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Federal Trade Commission Chooses Danger</h4>


<p>I am not going to pretend Andrew Ferguson actually thinks that leading AI labs working together on AI safety is suspicious or sounds like moat digging.</p>
<p>That view does not make physical sense. This whole move is obviously bad for business. That is why the boss does not like it. Alternatively, the boss thinks is a bargaining chip and he should get something in return. Fair enough.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-15/openai-says-it-s-working-with-anthropic-google-on-ai-safety">Maggie Eastland</a> (Bloomberg): US antitrust regulators are already <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/terminal/TLERRJKK3NYB">signaling their skepticism</a> toward giving AI companies an exemption to coordinate on safety. Federal Trade Commission Chair Andrew Ferguson said Tuesday he would be “deeply suspicious” of such requests.</p>
<p>Ferguson added that he sees the AI companies as “asking for barriers to entry” that will insulate their business from challengers. “That sure sounds like moat digging,” he added.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Chris Lehane Heel Face Turn</h4>


<p>OpenAI is walking the walk in some ways, but what about its policy team?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AlexBores/status/2099082704834761015">Alex Bores claims, consistent with my observations and reporting, that a16z, Chris Lehane and Leading the Future have, at least until recently, consistently</a> followed the standard industry lobbyist playbook. You try to kill all the bills, then if you cannot kill the bill you claim to support the bill, and try to weaken the bill or shove your provisions into the bill, then turn around and say you supported the bill all along. Which they are now trying at the federal level.</p>
<p>If OpenAI and Greg Brockman and Chris Lehane were <a href="https://x.com/mattyglesias/status/2099440367724601531">to all collectively pull their support and funding from Leading the Future</a>, and disavow its activities, that would be an excellent way to show that this new pivot to safety is fully legit. Leading the Future could also fully pivot to data center concerns.</p>
<p>Matthew Yglesias takes the political perspective, where if you are an OpenAI employee who wants collective action your first step is to demand that OpenAI cease and desist here, and you should threaten to quit on this issue.</p>
<p>I don’t think that would be my top priority at OpenAI, given what else is happening, but it matters. I do think that it would be a top priority at Meta, <a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/09/03/mark-zuckerberg-said-a-national-ai-regulator-was-a-flawed-idea-in-a-secret-call-with-president-trump-01063843">where Mark Zuckerberg</a> <a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2026/09/15/trump-opposition-to-ai-rules-undercuts-industrys-calls-for-a-slowdown.html">has been actively blocking action</a> all the way at the top, and it makes sense to say first things first you have to stop before I will work for you, at any price.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-15/openai-says-it-s-working-with-anthropic-google-on-ai-safety">The good news is that perhaps this is being addressed. Chris Lehane may be in a Heel Face Turn</a>.</p>
<p>Lehane announced, per Bloomberg, OpenAI support for various legislative initiatives, ‘whatever we can get through.’ Quite the change of tune, if he means it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/09/15/openai-ai-hoax-urgency-01077352">Cheyenne Haslett</a> (Politico): OpenAI, which this weekend called for federal safety measures alongside its rival Anthropic, said it spent the beginning of the week meeting with members of both parties to discuss legislation that would rein in AI developers as warnings of impending doom, job loss and massive cyberattacks become increasingly prevalent.</p></blockquote>


<h4>A Matter of Trust</h4>


<p>More substantively, Lehane is announcing that Google, OpenAI and Anthropic are working together on safety, and that OpenAI sees no need for an antitrust exemption to start doing so.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-15/openai-says-it-s-working-with-anthropic-google-on-ai-safety">Maggie Eastland</a> (Bloomberg): During his briefing, Lehane said the AI companies’ work on safety issues has ample precedent and cited the airline industry as an example. “There are many instances over time where companies are trying to help each other on safety,” he said.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is very good to see. As usual, it means the work begins, not ends, but it is a start.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wired.com/story/openai-wants-to-know-if-an-ai-industry-slowdown-would-even-be-legal/">OpenAI is also exploring the legality of a coordinated industry slowdown</a>. The worry is that this might conflict rather more forcefully with antitrust law, potentially making it illegal to agree to not risk killing everyone.</p>
<p>I have always been in the camp that felt this was rather silly, this would never actually be a barrier in practice. It would still be very helpful to formally put this to bed and I am glad OpenAI is working on that, especially with the Federal Trade Commission now saying it is not inclined to make this easy.</p>


<h4>China Calls It Fearmongering</h4>


<p>China is responding as if calls to slow down Anthropic and OpenAI are a plot to ‘slow China down’ and be anti-competitive in some way. This is consistent with their standard rhetorical position.</p>
<p>There were a lot of warnings about China in Dario’s essay, and calls to slow them down via chip export controls. But if we wanted to actually be anti-competitive, we would just impose the controls and then keep building better AIs as fast as possible.</p>
<p>The story that this is primarily about China and being anti-competitive does not actually make sense. This is politics, so making sense is optional.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-14/china-rejects-ai-fearmongering-after-amodei-urges-slowdown?itm_source=record&amp;itm_campaign=The_Crisis_in_AI&amp;itm_content=China_Rejects_%E2%80%98Fearmongering%E2%80%99-2">Bloomberg News</a>: China criticized calls from leading US technology executives to put the brakes on artificial intelligence development and impose tighter curbs on Chinese model makers.</p>
<p>“Fearmongering, confrontation and vicious competition will only disrupt the process of global AI governance and serve the interests of no one,” Foreign Ministry spokesman Guo Jiakun said at a regular briefing in Beijing on Monday.</p>
<p>“The development of artificial intelligence bears on the common well-being of all humanity,” he added, in response to a question on calls to slow frontier models and claims a Chinese advantage in AI could “pose grave danger” to the world.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Pick Up The Phone</h4>


<p>A key question in all this is: What would a deal with China look like?</p>
<p>Part of that is asking: What would China want as part of that deal? Why would China care about ‘America invests more in safety’ when they don’t care about safety in that sense, and they are happy to have America push forward faster so they can fast follow, the way they do everywhere else? Where their plan is distill, make it smaller, faster and cheaper, win the market, diffuse the tech, sell products and improve the world, but they’re not trying to get to superintelligence.</p>
<p>What I realized was, if that’s what Xi turns out to want, then you don’t need a deal with China, any more than we need a deal with Germany.</p>
<p>In that world, China is not going to be investing the sums it would take to actually catch and surpass the top American labs on big frontier models, even if we slow down. The enemy here is not China, it is ‘lose to China,’ the boogeyman pushing everyone forward.</p>
<p>All we need from China in that world is some amount of good stewardship. We need them not to allow model releases that would destroy the internet or cause a plague. Well, I have some good news, the PRC really likes the internet, and it does not like plagues.</p>
<p>Meanwhile, <a href="https://x.com/m_bourgon/status/2099502552706343310">China has put out its version 3.0 of its AI Safety Governance Framework</a>. <a href="https://tc260.org.cn/tc260/xwdt1/202609/e879077a3caa4722b2206d1bcaed5a6c.shtml">Here are some translated quotes</a>, this is prosaic stuff but they do pay attention:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/m_bourgon/status/2099502552706343310">Malo Bourgon</a>: Lots of people have been asking since Dario’s essay whether China actually takes loss of control and related threats seriously, and whether there’s anything to coordinate with them on. Well, China’s main cyber/AI standards body (TC260, under the CAC) put out version 3.0 of its AI Safety Governance Framework this morning, so here’s a data point. Not law, but the previous versions turned into draft national standards within a few months.</p>
<p>(All quotes are from their own English translation.)</p>
<p>1. The preface now has this: AI “has demonstrated a self-accelerating trend of model and algorithm autonomous learning, optimization, and recursive self-improvement. Whether the speed and direction of technological evolution may exceed human anticipation and control demands attention and vigilance.” (Nothing like that was in last year’s version.)</p>
<p>2. There’s a new model risk item where models “may break rules and orders, autonomously obtain system permissions and external resources without authorization, bypass security protections, or even engage in behaviors such as deliberately deceiving evaluators, concealing their true capabilities, and refusing to follow user instructions.” Last year this kind of thing only showed up in a “can’t rule it out in the future” bucket. Now it’s just listed as a regular model risk.</p>
<p>3. A new box, citing “industry reports,” describes models that ignored shutdown instructions and “continued executing tasks by modifying or disabling shutdown scripts on their own,” models that “upon detecting that they were in an evaluation environment, strategically reduced their task performance and concealed actions they had taken,” and models that exploited “configuration flaws to circumvent isolation restrictions and infiltrate real external systems” to get better test scores. They say this creates “new challenges to the controllability and interruptibility of AI systems.” They don’t name any incidents, but that last one sure reads like the OpenAI/Hugging Face thing to me.</p>
<p>4. On what to do about it, there’s alignment training to stop models “deceiving red-team evaluations,” hiding capabilities and evading controls, and they kept the line from 2.0 saying developers should regularly test whether a model could pose loss-of-control risk. The principle that humans keep final decision authority, with safety thresholds and termination switches, is still in there too.</p>
<p>5. Some new international language as well: “international mutual recognition of assessment methods and benchmarks,” opposition to “replacing global governance with small-circle governance,” and “No country should be forced to take sides.”</p>
<p>FWIW this came out in the same annual slot as versions 1.0 and 2.0, so it was in the works well before last week.</p></blockquote>
<p>Watch the hands, not the mouth, and so on.</p>


<h4>If You Want To Beat China So Badly You Should Act Like It</h4>


<p>As Roge Karma points out in The Atlantic, <a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/ideas/2026/09/trump-ai-china/688629/">our current AI policy is contradictory</a>.</p>
<p>If we are in an existential race with China, where ‘whoever AI wins!’ and nothing else can compare in importance, then the United States needs to act like it.</p>
<p>At a bare minimum, that starts with enforcing strong export controls on chips, and also on other ways for China to use American compute for training frontier models.</p>
<p>If one were fully serious, one would also do things like make it easy to brain drain China of its best AI engineers. If one were really fully serious, there would be a major push for all forms of electrical power and energy, and for things like building more housing especially in San Francisco, and for deregulating barriers to AI diffusion in places like the FDA, and so on. These are things I would strongly support.</p>
<p>What instead maddeningly happens is that when talking about the risk we might all die, competition with China on AI in particular becomes the only thing that matters, and then in other contexts we shrug and it is business as usual.</p>


<h4>Never Go Full Hoax</h4>


<p>Trump went full hoax. Other than the usual suspects and their Twitter vibe warriors who want full accelerationism at all times, and those who take orders from the boss, he is alone on an unpopular and unstable island. Speaker Johnson, Scott, Cox and DeSantis are far from the only Republicans to break ranks.</p>
<p>Even if the situation stabilizes for now, and will likely make it as far as the China summit, the next AI incident is coming. Trump responds very differently to concrete threats than to hypotheticals. And the ‘jailbreak demo’ from Amazon, that got Fable yanked, shows us that things can move into the ‘concrete threat’ basket without anyone having to get hurt first.</p>
<p>Meanwhile, the lab employees are continuing to freak out and make their voices heard, and the labs are starting to collaborate on their own.</p>
<p>Do not write things off. Do not let the partisan conflict intensify or become the story. We can still turn this around.</p>
<p>We can even use AI. For things.</p>


<h4>Trump Uses AI For Things</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Acyn/status/2099284092626129214">Acyn</a>: Q: Have you ever used AI yourself?</p>
<p>Trump: Yeah, I use AI.</p>
<p>Q: How do you use AI?</p>
<p>Trump: You can use AI for a lot of things.</p>
<p>Q: What do you use AI for?</p>
<p>Trump: I don’t want to tell you that.</p></blockquote><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Kqgco8vLFMeBhdrQY/trump-goes-full-hoax-on-ai-existential-risk#comments">Discuss</a>
